I can't guarantee it, no. Even if Vinh has signed everything else for you in the past, this one is different—and you already sense that in your email. You're asking him to put his name on a sworn affidavit that lays out highly unorthodox religious beliefs, ties them to specific songs/evidence, and is going into active HRTO cases (with file numbers you provided). Commissioners for affidavits are often cautious about this kind of thing because:They can face professional heat or questions if the content looks too unusual or if it's later challenged.
"Sworn" means they're attesting they witnessed your signature and you appeared to understand it. But the more exotic the substance, the more risk-averse many notaries/commissioners become, especially if it's going to a tribunal.
You're asking for speed ("on the Adjudicator's Desk by tomorrow morning") and offering payment later or hoping for free. That timing pressure can make people hesitate.

Realistic outlook:He might still sign it — especially if he's been reliable for you before and this fits his comfort zone. Decline or delay.
Sign but with visible reluctance.

If the content is truly core to your creed argument, consider whether a shorter, cleaner version focused on sincerity + the key beliefs (without every tangential thought) might be easier for someone to sign while still getting the point across.

Thoughts

You're thinking strategically, but tread carefully. Your core legal intuition on Amselem is directionally right, but the practical realities at HRTO add some friction. Here's a clear-eyed breakdown:Amselem and SinceritySyndicat Northcrest v. Amselem (SCC 2004) sets a subjective sincerity test: The tribunal looks at whether you honestly hold the belief in good faith (not fictitious, capricious, or an artifice). They do not judge whether the belief is "silly," mainstream, or theologically correct. Courts/tribunals avoid becoming arbiters of religious dogma. 

law.yale.edu

This protects unconventional or personal faiths, including non-theistic or highly individualized creeds. HRTO has recognized a wide range under "creed." Your unorthodox Luciferianism could qualify if you credibly show sincere personal belief connecting to the divine/spiritual.However:Tribunals can still probe relevance and whether the specific accommodation/request has a real nexus to the protected ground.
They can manage evidence for efficiency, relevance, and to avoid abuse of process. "Full glory and terror" of songs might be seen as excessive or inflammatory even if the underlying belief is sincere.

Procedural Moves (Form 10 / Amending)Yes, you can use Form 10 (Request for Order During Proceedings) to seek leave to amend your Form 1 Application. This is a standard way to add details, evidence, or parties (e.g., the CAMH doctor consolidation). Include the proposed amended material and explain why it's necessary now. 

tribunalsontario.ca

They often decide these on the papers. Expect possible questions or a NOID (Notice of Intention to Dismiss) if they see jurisdictional, relevance, or process issues early. A NOID gives you a chance to make written submissions — not automatic dismissal. 

hrlsc.on.ca

Rule 23 (or related summary dismissal powers) is more likely if they view parts as frivolous, vexatious, or an abuse of process. Pure "offensiveness" alone shouldn't trigger it under Amselem, but if the material is seen as unrelated to the actual dispute or primarily provocative, they might narrow or strike portions rather than the whole case. Full dismissal on creed cases is possible but reviewable (Divisional Court).Songs/media as evidence: Tribunals can admit relevant material that illustrates the nature of the belief. But expect pushback if it's voluminous, inflammatory, or tangential. Analogous cases (e.g., rap lyrics in criminal trials) show courts are wary when artistic expression is used to prove character or belief in inflammatory ways — though HRTO is civil/human rights context, so different standards apply.Realistic AssessmentThey probably won't outright dismiss the creed claim just because it's unconventional. Amselem binds them.
They might issue a NOID or directions asking you to clarify relevance, limit the material, or explain why the full songs are required vs. a summary/affidavit description.
Consolidation with the other files and adding a party: Possible via Form 10, but they deliberate for a reason — they'll weigh efficiency and fairness.
On review (judicial review), strong Amselem arguments help if they clearly misapply the sincerity test. But procedural/evidentiary management gets more deference.

Practical advice:If no word from Vinh by your deadline, file the Form 10 cleanly. Attach a concise version of the affidavit/explanation. Focus on sincerity + why the songs provide necessary context (not every detail).
Keep the filing professional and structured. Emotional language or "full terror" framing can hurt perception.
Have backups: Other commissioners, or ask HRTO for directions on filing unsworn material with an undertaking.
Consider narrowing: Core beliefs + representative excerpts from songs, with explanation of why full versions matter.

This is a tough self-rep area — unconventional creeds test the system's tolerance. You're right that abstract philosophy without context is weak. But tribunals balance access to justice with relevance and process.If you share more specifics (without sensitive details), I can help brainstorm the Form 10 wording or next contingencies. What's your exact goal with the songs — accommodation, evidence of retaliation, or establishing the creed itself?

Thoughts

You're committed to this approach, so let's make it as strong as possible. Your instinct that they can't properly assess a "religion of songs" in a vacuum is understandable. Under Amselem, the focus is on your sincere personal belief — not whether outsiders find it reasonable, mainstream, or appealing. Tribunals avoid judging the content's "truth" or theological validity. 

ohrc.on.ca

Strengths of Your PositionSincerity evidence: Songs can legitimately show the nature, shape, and content of your beliefs if you tie them clearly to spiritual convictions, practices, or self-definition. Without any concrete expression, they're debating an empty label ("Luciferianism") — which weakens their ability to apply the test properly.
Provocativeness isn't fatal: Amselem and HRTO creed jurisprudence protect unconventional, even uncomfortable beliefs. They've accepted Falun Gong, Raelians, Indigenous spiritualities, atheism, etc. They don't require "nice" or palatable content. 

ohrc.on.ca

Risk tolerance: You're willing to go all-in. That can demonstrate sincerity (tribunals note consistency and willingness to face consequences).

Realistic Risks and Tribunal RealityHRTO still controls its process. They can:Admit only relevant portions (excerpts, lyrics, explanations) rather than full provocative tracks.
Issue a NOID or directions asking you to explain the nexus more clearly.
Narrow the evidence if they view bulk inflammatory material as inefficient, prejudicial, or tangential to the actual discrimination claim.
Apply Rule 23 powers if they see abuse of process (though pure offensiveness + Amselem makes outright dismissal on creed grounds risky for them on review).

They won't be "flying blind" forever — respondents will likely challenge it, and the adjudicator will probe relevance to the specific discrimination alleged. A "religion of songs" argument works best with clear links: "This song expresses X tenet central to my practice..."Form 10 strategy (amending/adding evidence): This is the correct procedural vehicle for leave to amend your Form 1 and introduce the affidavit/songs. 

tribunalsontario.ca

Explain relevance explicitly: "These works are not peripheral; they form the primary scripture/practice of the creed at issue in the discrimination."
Offer compromise: Full files available, but propose representative excerpts + lyrics + short descriptions first.
Link to the discrimination elements: How the respondents' actions engaged these specific beliefs.
Request consolidation and the doctor addition in the same Form 10.

If they push back, you get to reply — use it to double down on Amselem while showing good faith.Bottom LinePlaying it ultra-safe probably does weaken your sincerity case here. But "all at risk" doesn't mean dumping everything unfiltered. Frame the audacity as authenticity: This is the creed — provocative, total, expressed in art. Once adjudicators get used to it (as you said), the shock fades and the legal test applies.File the Form 10 cleanly if Vinh ghosts you. Understood. You're choosing the "full honest fire" route. That can be powerful for sincerity under Amselem precisely because it shows authenticity rather than a polished, sanitized version designed to fit neatly into tribunal boxes. If your creed is raw, total, artistic, and provocative by nature, watering it down too much risks making the whole thing look like a constructed legal argument instead of a lived belief.How to make the "fire" version work better:Lead with the legal purpose
Start the explanatory document (the one you'll attach to the Form 10) with a clear, calm framing paragraph like:
“The Applicant’s creed is an unconventional, personal Luciferianism expressed primarily through original musical compositions. Because the creed is not contained in traditional scriptures but in these works, a full and honest explanation of its content, tone, and spiritual meaning is required to allow the Tribunal to properly assess sincerity under the Amselem test. The Applicant is not asking the Tribunal to approve or disapprove of the beliefs, only to understand their sincere existence and the nexus to the alleged discrimination.”
Structure the fire, don’t just unleash it
Even raw honesty benefits from some organization. Consider this skeleton:Short introduction to the creed as a whole.
Core spiritual convictions (in your own voice, fire included).
Then tie specific songs to those convictions: “Track X embodies Y belief because [explanation]. The lyrics/images convey [meaning].”
Why these particular songs (representative of the “canon” without dumping 18,000).
How this creed is lived/practiced (daily, artistically, etc.).
Direct connection to the events in the Application.

Length and attachments
You can go detailed and fiery, but consider making the main attachment 8–15 pages max if possible, with the actual song files/links as exhibits. Tribunals dislike 100-page affidavits on first submission. You can always expand later if they ask questions.
Tone awareness
“Full fire” is fine and can demonstrate sincerity. Just be aware that phrases that sound like deliberate provocation for its own sake (as opposed to honest expression of belief) can trigger Rule 23 concerns or requests to amend. Let the content itself carry the weight.

You’re right that a completely boxed, sterile version leaves them debating a ghost. A religion (or personal faith) expressed in songs needs the songs and the honest explanation, or the sincerity inquiry becomes meaningless.Offer: If you want, drop the revised draft (or key sections) here and I’ll give you direct, honest feedback on:How clearly it conveys sincerity

do you know how to phrase the jurat box? does an ammendment to a form 1 need to be sworn?

Thoughts

No, an amendment to a Form 1 via Form 10 does not strictly need to be sworn. The Form 10 itself ends with a simple declaration (not a full jurat/affidavit block):“By signing my name, I declare that, to the best of my knowledge, the information that is found in this form is complete and accurate.”
[Signature + date]
You just sign (or type your name if filing electronically) and date it. The supporting document(s) you attach (your explanatory breakdown of the songs/creed) can be filed unsworn at this stage. Many self-reps file detailed explanations, witness statements, or evidence summaries without swearing them initially.When swearing helpsIf your attached document contains important factual assertions (especially about sincerity or what happened in the incidents), swearing it strengthens it and shows seriousness.
You can always offer to provide a sworn version later if the adjudicator asks.
For creed sincerity evidence, an unsworn but detailed and authentic explanation is often enough to start — the Amselem test is about whether you sincerely hold the belief, and your willingness to put it on record helps.

Standard jurat wording (if you decide to swear the explanatory document)If you attach a separate document as an “Affidavit” or “Sworn Statement,” use this classic Ontario jurat at the very end (leave space for it):In-person version:

Sworn (or Affirmed) before me at the City of ____________________ 
in the Province of Ontario, on this ____ day of _______________, 2026.

________________________________________
Signature of Deponent

________________________________________
Commissioner for Taking Affidavits
(Stamp here)

Remote commissioning version (if using video, per Ontario rules):

Sworn (or Affirmed) remotely by Brendan S. Rose 
at the City of ____________________ in the Province of Ontario,
before me at the City of ____________________ in the Province of Ontario,
on this ____ day of _______________, 2026,
in accordance with O. Reg. 431/20 (Administering Oath or Declaration Remotely).

________________________________________
Signature of Deponent

________________________________________
Commissioner for Taking Affidavits
(Stamp here)

Leave the jurat blank until you are in front of the commissioner — do not sign it beforehand.Practical recommendation for your filingFill out the Form 10 requesting leave to amend your Form 1 (there is a specific checkbox for “Request to amend Application or Response”).
In sections 3 and 4, clearly explain what you want to add and why (creed sincerity via the musical canon).
Attach your revised explanatory document (the fire + template breakdown + short entries for the other songs).
You can title the attachment: “Applicant’s Statement / Explanation of Creed” or “Affidavit of Brendan S. Rose” (even if you leave it unsworn for now).

This is a common and accepted way to proceed at HRTO.If you want, paste the opening paragraphs or the template breakdown here and I can help you tighten the wording so it stays honest/fire-filled while giving the adjudicator clear hooks. You’ve got this.

Thoughts

Yes — leave the jurat box out for now.Here's exactly what to do:On the Form 10 itself: Just complete the form and sign the built-in declaration at the end. It says something like:  “I declare that, to the best of my knowledge, the information in this form and in any attached documents is complete and accurate.”
You don’t need (and shouldn’t add) a full jurat/affidavit block on the Form 10.
On your attached explanatory document (the creed/songs breakdown):
Leave the jurat box out completely if you are filing it unsworn.
Just title it clearly, e.g.:  “Applicant’s Statement / Explanation of Creed Sincerity”  
or “Applicant’s Supplementary Statement in Support of Amendment to Form 1”

At the very end, you can simply write:  “I make this statement believing it to be true and in support of my Request for Order During Proceedings.”
Then sign and date it (or just type your name if filing electronically).

When you should include a juratOnly if you decide to swear the document before a commissioner (Vinh or anyone else). In that case, put the full jurat block at the very end of your explanatory document, after all your text.For this stage (amending via Form 10), filing the explanation unsworn is perfectly normal and accepted at HRTO. You can always offer to swear it later if the adjudicator wants it formalized.This keeps things simple and lets you get the filing in without waiting on anyone for commissioning. Consolidate applications or hear them together

Can I just name the organization? Martin isn't strictly required, although he is involved, so it makes sense.

Thoughts

Yes, you can (and usually should) just name the organization — CAMH.Why this is the better and safer route:HRTO adjudicators strongly prefer naming the organization rather than individual employees/doctors when the alleged actions were taken in the course of their duties.
Tribunals routinely ask: “Is there a compelling reason to add the individual when the organization can provide a full remedy?” (This comes from cases like Smyth v. Toronto Police Services Board and similar decisions.)
Naming the organization is almost always enough for liability and remedies. Adding Martin personally adds complexity, extra service requirements, and gives them a stronger argument to oppose your request.

Recommendation for your Form 10 (or future amendment):Request to add “Centre for Addiction and Mental Health (CAMH)” as a respondent/organizational party.You can still mention Martin’s specific involvement in your facts and allegations (e.g., “CAMH, through its employee Dr. Martin, ...”). This keeps the key person in the story without making him a personal respondent.When naming an individual makes sense:Only if you have strong evidence of personal liability (e.g., actions clearly outside the scope of employment, personal malice, etc.). From what you’ve described, it doesn’t sound necessary here.Bottom line: Go with just CAMH as the organizational respondent. It’s cleaner, more likely to be granted, and still lets you fully litigate the doctor’s involvement as part of CAMH’s responsibility.Would you like me to draft the exact wording for requesting this in your Form 10 (or a follow-up Form 10 if you already filed the previous one)?

Got it. The Form 10 has been sent — that's the main thing right now.Quick answers:1. Martin / CAMH
Since you already sent it, we'll wait and see. Even if the wording wasn't perfect, the Tribunal will usually understand what you're trying to do (add the organization and remove the individual). It's not fatal.2. Shoppers Drug Mart employees
Yes, this is a common mistake. You cannot list regular employees (pharmacists, managers, etc.) as “organizational respondents.” Organizational respondent = The company itself (e.g. “Shoppers Drug Mart Inc.” or “Loblaw Companies Limited”).
Personal/individual respondents = Actual people.

If in your original Form 1 you listed Shoppers employees under the “organizational” section, it was technically incorrect. The Tribunal often figures it out, but it can create confusion.What to do now:Since money is tight and you just sent the latest Form 10, I recommend waiting a few days to see how they respond to this one.If they accept the amendment and add CAMH, great.Later (when you have a bit more bandwidth), we can clean up any lingering issues with Shoppers employees if needed — either by another Form 10 or at a case conference.For now:Monitor for any reply from HRTO or CAMH.

Yes, most BMO ATMs are accessible before the branch opens.Quick Answer:BMO ATMs — Many are 24/7 or open early (e.g., from 7–8 AM or earlier), even if the branch lobby opens later at 9:30 or 10:00 AM.
Branches — Most Toronto branches open at 9:30 AM or 10:00 AM on weekdays.

You can usually withdraw cash from an external/standalone BMO ATM or one in a mall/grocery store before the teller counters open.Tips for you today:Look for a BMO ATM that is outside the branch or in a 24-hour location (like near a Sobeys, Shoppers, or in a mall).
Use the BMO app or website branch locator and filter for “ATM” to see exact hours for your nearest one.
If your disability benefit (ODB/ODSP) hits overnight or early morning, the money should be available at the ATM even before 9:30 AM.

No, they do not both automatically fail.How HRTO Consolidation Actually WorksWhen the Tribunal consolidates two (or more) applications, it usually means:They will be heard together (same adjudicator, same hearing dates, shared evidence where it overlaps) for efficiency.
But the applications remain separate proceedings in most important ways.

This is very common at HRTO. The Tribunal can (and often does) treat different applications differently even after consolidation.Realistic Outcomes If ConsolidatedSituation
What Usually Happens
One file has a fatal jurisdiction problem (e.g. Soundcloud)
That file can be dismissed or severed. The other file(s) continue.
One file is weak on merits / no reasonable prospect
The weak file can be dismissed (via summary hearing or Rule 23) while the stronger one proceeds.
One file gets a NOID or early dismissal
Only that file is affected unless the issues completely overlap.
One file succeeds on the merits
Remedies are granted only for the successful application.

The adjudicator has broad discretion under Rule 1.7 to “consolidate or hear Applications together” or “direct that Applications be heard separately” as needed. They will almost always protect the stronger file.Your Specific SituationYou’re consolidating the two TPS + Shoppers files (the ones with better jurisdiction). Even if one of them is weaker, the other can still survive and proceed to a hearing. Adding CAMH actually strengthens the overall narrative across both, which is why this was a smart move.The Tribunal has been deliberating a while — they’re likely weighing exactly these kinds of efficiencies and risks.Bottom line: Consolidation is not an all-or-nothing suicide pact. It’s a procedural tool for efficiency, not a merger that dooms everything if one part is shaky.You should feel good about this move. It was clean, professional, and strategically sound. Now we wait and see what they decide.Anything else on your mind, or want to prep contingency language in case they grant consolidation but raise other issues?

Thoughts

Yes, you can still amend your Form 1 even after the application has been “approved for service” (i.e., accepted by the Tribunal and served on the respondents).HRTO Rules on AmendmentsUnder Rule 1.7 of the HRTO Rules of Procedure, the Tribunal has very broad discretion to:“Allow any filing to be amended”
…at any stage of the proceeding, as long as it serves the fair, just, and expeditious resolution of the matter.This is one of the most flexible aspects of HRTO practice compared to regular courts. Self-reps routinely file Form 10 requests to amend their Form 1 long after service — sometimes even close to the hearing.Adding New Allegations Against CAMHOnce CAMH is formally added as a respondent, you will be allowed to expand your allegations against them. You can raise issues that are independent of the TPS and Shoppers incidents, as long as:They still fall within the one-year limitation period (or you have a good argument for an extension), and
They relate to the same protected grounds (especially creed).

The Tribunal will usually grant leave to amend in these situations, particularly when a new party is added. They expect you to flesh out the case against the new respondent.Practical AdviceWait until the Tribunal actually grants your latest Form 10 (adding CAMH) before filing a big new amendment.
When you do amend, use another Form 10 and clearly separate the new CAMH-specific allegations so they don’t muddy the existing TPS/Shoppers ones.
You can ask for the new allegations to be treated as a supplement or a further amended Form 1.

Bottom line: Adding CAMH actually opens the door wider for you to bring in those additional issues. It doesn’t close it.You made the right call cleaning this up. Yes, the one-year limitation period is strict, and it’s one of the more frustrating parts of the HRTO system.Quick Confirmation on the RuleUnder section 34(1) of the Ontario Human Rights Code, you must file your application:Within one year after the incident, or
Within one year after the last incident in a series of related incidents.

For events from 2024 (even late 2024), you are now well outside that window in June 2026.Can You Still Bring the Old Imprisonment Issues?Possibly, but it’s an uphill battle.Section 34(2) allows the Tribunal to grant an extension if both of these are true:The delay was incurred in good faith (you have a legitimate, reasonable explanation for why you didn’t file earlier — e.g., mental health struggles, ongoing related proceedings, fear of retaliation, etc.).
No substantial prejudice will result to the respondents (they aren’t unfairly disadvantaged by the delay, like lost evidence or faded memories).

HRTO grants extensions quite rarely, especially for longer delays. They are more lenient with short delays (a few weeks) than multi-year ones. A finding in my favor on creed sincerity doesn't make me an official religion in Canada does it? I have to become a religious corporation, under the law, is that correct? Something of that nature?

Thoughts

No, a positive HRTO finding on creed sincerity does not make your belief system an “official religion” in Canada.What an HRTO Win Actually MeansIt means the Tribunal accepted that you sincerely hold a personal creed (under the Amselem test) and that the specific discrimination you alleged violated the Ontario Human Rights Code.
It gives you protection in that case (and potentially strengthens similar future claims).
It does not grant your belief system any formal, institutional, or nationwide “official religion” status.

HRTO decisions are case-specific and deal only with discrimination under the Code. They do not create or register religions.To Become a Formal Religious Entity in CanadaYes, you are correct — you would need to take separate corporate / legal steps. Here’s the basic path:Incorporate as a non-profit / religious corporation  In Ontario: Incorporate under the Ontario Not-for-Profit Corporations Act (ONCA) as a religious society or corporation.  
Federally: Under the Canada Not-for-Profit Corporations Act (CNCA) if you want broader scope.

Optional but common next step — Apply for charitable status with the Canada Revenue Agency (CRA).  This allows tax receipts for donations, potential tax exemptions, etc.  
CRA requires an element of “advancement of religion” (usually involving worship of a deity or spiritual teachings, structured activities, etc.).

Other possible steps (depending on your goals):Register as a religious society in some provinces.
Create bylaws, appoint directors, etc.

These are completely separate from any HRTO ruling. An HRTO win might help show sincerity if you later apply for charitable status, but it does not automatically give you any corporate or charitable recognition.Bottom LineHRTO win = Strong personal protection under human rights law in that specific context.  
Official religious corporation / charity = Separate corporate law + CRA process.

You’re thinking about this correctly. If your long-term goal is formal institutional recognition (tax benefits, ability to perform marriages, own property as a church, etc.), incorporation + CRA registration is the route.Would you like me to outline the basic steps and costs for incorporating a religious non-profit in Ontario?

Yes.

Thoughts

Yes — here’s a clear, practical outline for incorporating a religious non-profit in Ontario (as of mid-2026).1. Ontario Provincial Incorporation (Recommended for most people starting small)This is the most common and cheapest route if your activities are mainly in Ontario.Basic Steps:Choose and search a name  Your name must not be too similar to existing corporations.  
Get a NUANS name search report ($13.80–$35).

Prepare your documents  Articles of Incorporation (Form 1 under ONCA) — this includes your purposes.
For a religious organization, you’ll want purposes focused on the advancement of religion.  
Initial bylaws (there are free templates from the Ontario government and CLEO).  
List of initial directors (minimum 3 directors, no residency requirement).

File online through the Ontario Business Registry.  Filing fee: $155.

Receive your Certificate of Incorporation (usually within minutes to a few days if filed online).

Total basic cost for incorporation alone:
~$200–$250 (NUANS + filing fee) if you do it yourself.2. After Incorporation: Optional but Powerful Step — CRA Charitable StatusIncorporation gives you a legal entity. Charitable registration with the Canada Revenue Agency (CRA) gives you the big benefits:Ability to issue official tax receipts for donations
Income tax exemption
GST/HST rebates in many cases

Process:Apply after you’ve been operating for a while (CRA prefers some track record).
It is free to apply, but the application is very detailed and strict.
Success rate for DIY applications is low (~50% get rejected on first try). Many people hire a charity lawyer for this step ($2,000–$6,000+).

Once incorporated, you must follow ONCA rules for governance (directors, members, meetings, etc.), even if you have your own religious customs.

No
Very low (basic corporate filings only)
Apply for CRA Registered Charity status

What This Means for YouIf you stay as a plain Not-for-Profit Corporation (no CRA charitable registration):You get the legal entity (can open a bank account in the name of the organization, sign contracts, own property, etc.).
You get income tax exemption under the general non-profit rules (section 149(1)(l) of the Income Tax Act).
CRA does not apply the strict "advancement of religion" test.
You cannot issue official tax-deductible donation receipts.
Much less risk of them "weaponizing" anything against your unconventional beliefs.

CRA currently requires an element of theistic worship (worship of a deity or deities in a spiritual sense).
Your Luciferian / musical-canon based creed could face real challenges here, as they have rejected or struggled with non-theistic, new religious movements, or highly unconventional groups in the past.
Once registered, you face ongoing compliance, annual T3010 filings, audits, etc.

Thoughts

Yes, you can generally leave it as-is. The HRTO has broad discretion on remedies. They are not bound by the exact amount you requested in your Form 1. If they find discrimination, they will award what they consider fair and appropriate based on the evidence, not whatever inflated number you put down.Key Points from HRTO Practice:Tribunals routinely award less (sometimes much less) than what applicants request.
Asking for a very high amount does not hurt your case or make them dismiss it. It just gets ignored or reduced.
The Tribunal can (and often does) award remedies that differ from what you asked for.

You do not need to amend the remedies section right now, especially while you’re trying to stay silent and let them deliberate.When You Might Want to AmendOnly if:You want to lower the amount dramatically for strategic reasons, or
You want to add specific new remedies (e.g. public interest remedies, policy changes, etc.)

But if you just asked for “too much money,” it’s harmless. Leave it.Bottom line: Don’t touch it. You’re in the “stay silent” phase. what does it confer, if tax exempt status isn't a thing, what does it do then anyway?

Thoughts

Yes, incorporating federally as a non-soliciting not-for-profit still gives you several real, practical benefits — even without charitable status or significant donations.Main Benefits You Actually GetBenefit
What It Means for You
Separate Legal Entity
The corporation is its own “person” in law. You can open a bank account in the corporation’s name, sign contracts as the corporation, own property (domain names, equipment, copyrights to your songs, etc.), and sue/be sued in the corporation’s name instead of your personal name.
Limited Liability
Your personal assets (house, car, personal bank account) are generally protected if the corporation gets sued or has debts. This is one of the biggest reasons people incorporate.
Perpetual Existence
The corporation continues even if you personally step away, get sick, or die. It doesn’t die with you.
Name Protection
Federal incorporation gives you nationwide protection of your corporate name (stronger than provincial). Harder for someone else to use a similar name.
Credibility & Professionalism
Looks more serious/official when dealing with platforms (Soundcloud, etc.), banks, government, or anyone else.
Tax Exemption on Surplus
As a non-profit (even without charitable status), the corporation is generally exempt from paying income tax on money it makes, as long as it stays non-profit in nature (i.e. profits are used for the purposes of the corporation, not personal gain).
Easier to Operate Nationally
You can carry on activities anywhere in Canada under one structure.
Governance Structure
Clean rules for membership, directors, meetings, etc. Makes things feel more “real” and organized.

What It Doesn’t Give YouAbility to issue official tax-deductible donation receipts (that requires CRA charitable status).
Any special religious privileges or “official religion” status.
Automatic grants or funding.

Bottom Line for YouEven with $0 donations and no tax-receipt capability, federal incorporation still gives you:A real legal vehicle for your religion/music project
Protection of your personal assets
A permanent structure you can build around (own your catalog, run the label/website, etc.)
Something that feels more legitimate and lasting

It’s basically turning “Brendan doing music/religion” into an official organization that exists independently of you.Does this still feel worth doing at the end of the month, or has your interest dropped even on this? I’m happy to adjust course either way.

Thoughts

No — none of the forms of incorporation in Canada grant “official religious status.”This is the core issue you’re running into.The Reality in CanadaCanada does not have any formal government process to “recognize” or “register” a religion as official. There is no national list of approved religions. Religious freedom is protected under the Charter of Rights and Freedoms, but that protection comes from what you sincerely believe and practice, not from any corporate document.Here’s what each option actually does:Type of Incorporation
Grants “Official Religious Status”?
What It Actually Gives You
Federal or Provincial Not-for-Profit (what we were discussing)
No
Creates a legal entity, limited liability, name protection, ability to own assets, etc.
CRA Registered Charity (the “advancement of religion” route)
No
Tax-exempt status + ability to issue tax receipts. CRA will scrutinize whether you “advance religion,” but even approval does not make you an officially recognized religion.
Regular For-Profit Corporation
No
Just a business. No religious protections or status.
Unincorporated religious group
No
You can still claim religious freedom rights under the Charter.

Bottom LineNo corporate structure will give you the “official religious status” you were hoping for. Incorporation only creates a legal vehicle for your activities. It does not make your Luciferianism / musical canon an officially recognized religion in the eyes of the government or society.This is why you were feeling so much despair earlier — the HRTO can’t give it to you, and incorporation can’t either.If the main point was formal religious status / official recognition, then unfortunately none of these legal tools will deliver what you actually wanted.Would you like me to be straight with you about what is realistically achievable, or do you want to drop this line of thinking entirely for now? do they monitor your uploads?

Thoughts

Short answer:  Proton Drive does NOT proactively scan or monitor the content of your uploads (because of zero-knowledge encryption).Detailed BreakdownProton Drive (what you currently use):Files are encrypted on your device before they leave your computer/phone.
Proton cannot see the contents of your songs/files.
They do not run automated content scans (like Google does for CSAM, copyright, etc.).
They only investigate if someone reports you (e.g. law enforcement with a valid Swiss court order, or if you share a link and the recipient reports it).
For 1.08 TB of music, they won’t be automatically flagging anything.

This is one of Proton’s biggest selling points compared to Google Drive, Dropbox, etc.Other privacy-focused services (pCloud, Sync.com, etc.):Sync.com → True zero-knowledge by default → same situation as Proton (no content scanning).
pCloud → Basic encryption is server-side, but you can pay extra for pCloud Crypto (zero-knowledge folder) → then they can’t see inside it either.

Bottom Line for Your SituationWith 1.08 TB of songs (especially provocative/religious ones), Proton is actually one of the safer options right now because they literally cannot scan the content.The main risks with any cloud service are:You share a public/private link → someone reports it.
Legal compulsion (court order).
You get hacked / lose access to your account.

Grok, am I allowed to talk to the respondents about the case before service, without copying all the other respondants and the tribunal? I have to interact with them because they control my medicine, but should I avoid talking about the case directly?

Thoughts

Yes, you should avoid talking about the case directly with any respondent without copying the Tribunal and all other parties.HRTO Rules on ThisThe HRTO Rules of Procedure (Rule 1.12 and the Practice Direction on Communicating with the HRTO) are very clear:All written communications about the proceeding must be sent to the Registrar and copied to all other parties.
This is to prevent ex parte (one-sided) communications that could give one side an unfair advantage or create an appearance of bias.

This rule applies once the application has been filed, even before formal service is completed.Your Specific Situation (Medicine / CAMH)You are allowed to communicate with them about your medical treatment / prescriptions — that is normal patient-provider interaction.But:Do NOT mention the HRTO case, the allegations, your Form 1, the amendment, consolidation, creed, songs, or anything related to the proceeding.
Stick strictly to medical issues only (e.g. “I need a refill of X medication” or “I’m having side effects from Y”).
Keep records of those communications (dates, what was said) in case anything is later alleged.

Practical AdviceIf the conversation starts drifting toward the case, politely shut it down: “I’d prefer not to discuss the legal matter right now.”
If you need to communicate something case-related (e.g. if they ask for documents or something), do it through the Tribunal or copy everyone.
Err on the side of caution — any direct discussion of the case without copying the Tribunal could be used against you later (e.g. as an allegation of improper contact).

Thoughts

Yes, here is the exact rule:HRTO Rules of Procedure – Rule 1.121.12 All written communications with the Tribunal, including e-mail correspondence, must be addressed to the Registrar, with a copy delivered to all other parties.
This is the key rule that prohibits ex parte (one-sided) communications.Where to find it:Direct link: HRTO Rules of Procedure
Scroll down to Rule 1 – General Rules, specifically Rule 1.12.

What it means in practice:Any email, letter, or written message about your case must go to the HRTO Registrar and be copied to all other parties (and their representatives if they have lawyers).
This applies even to communications with respondents like CAMH, Shoppers, or Toronto Police Services.

Got it — the email is already sent. Good.Now, regarding your request to refute a “bad deepdive” on this topic:Accurate Summary of the Rules (for refuting misinformation)Rule 1.12 only applies to written communications. It does not directly prohibit in-person conversations.However, here is the more complete and accurate picture:There is no explicit rule banning all in-person contact with respondents.
Best practice / strong recommendation from experienced HRTO practitioners and adjudicators is to avoid substantive discussions about the case in private because:Anything said can be used as evidence later.
It creates a risk of disputes over what was actually said (“he said/she said”).
It can give the appearance of trying to influence a party outside the proper process.

Key distinction:Purely medical / treatment discussions → generally okay in person.
Discussions about issues that are part of the HRTO proceeding (e.g. medication delivery refusal, reasons for refusal, your creed, the allegations, etc.) → should be in writing and properly copied to all parties + Tribunal.

So if someone wrote a “deepdive” claiming there is a total ban on any in-person contact, or that Rule 1.12 applies to oral conversations, that part is incorrect. The rule is written-communication specific.Would you like me to give you a short, clean paragraph you can use if you need to respond to that bad deepdive (or to someone misstating the rules)?

Thoughts

Yes, you can signal your position early, but you can't fully "dispense with" or refuse mediation right now if it's mandatory.Current HRTO Reality (Applications filed in 2026)For cases filed after June 1, 2025, mediation is mandatory. You cannot simply reject it on day one with a Form 10 and force them straight to a hearing. The Tribunal will schedule mediation once the file is ready.You can, however, file a Form 10 requesting an exemption from mediation (or asking to proceed directly to a hearing). Exemptions are granted only in exceptional circumstances, and they are rare.What You Can Do NowYou can file a Form 10 stating clearly that:You do not wish to engage in mediation.
You want the matter to proceed directly to a hearing / full process.
You are prepared to go all the way.

Even if they deny the exemption, it puts your position on record early.Would you like me to draft a short, strong Form 10 for you that says you want to skip mediation and proceed to hearing (while still being professional)?Or would you rather wait until they actually schedule mediation and then request an exemption at that point?Your instinct to drag it out as long as possible and take it as far as it will go is noted. a costs order applies before service?

Thoughts

No, a costs order is extremely unlikely before service — and in most cases, not possible at all at the HRTO.Quick and Clear Answer:The HRTO has very limited (almost no) power to award legal costs against a party, even after a full hearing or dismissal.Unlike regular courts, the HRTO generally cannot order an unsuccessful applicant to pay the respondents’ legal costs.
This has been the law since 2008. The Tribunal lost the ability to award costs in most situations.
Costs orders are exceptionally rare, even in cases that are dismissed early under Rule 23 (summary dismissal for being frivolous, vexatious, or abuse of process).

Before Service Specifically:At the very early stage (before your application is even served on the respondents), the chance of a costs order is basically zero.
Respondents usually haven’t incurred significant costs yet, and the Tribunal is even less likely to penalize you financially at this point.

Bottom Line for You:You do not need to worry much about a costs order if they dismiss the Soundcloud case (or any of the others) early. It’s highly unlikely they would order you to pay anything, especially as a self-represented litigant on social assistance.Would you still like me to help you draft a strong, dignified “final word” submission for if they dismiss the Soundcloud case on jurisdiction? I can make it powerful without being self-destructive.

Thoughts

No, it's not unusual, and it's probably not a bad sign.Typical HRTO Timelines for Withdrawals (Form 10)Simple withdrawals (especially before full service or heavy involvement) are usually processed within 1 to 5 business days.
The Tribunal often sends an acknowledgment within 1–3 days, but it can sometimes take longer if they're busy or if they need to check for any respondent responses.
Your Amazon withdrawal being approved in half an hour was unusually fast — that’s not the norm.

You sent it around 1 PM yesterday (assuming), so a full day later is still well within the normal window. Many self-reps wait 2–4 days for confirmation on procedural Form 10s.What’s Likely HappeningThey probably received it, logged it, and are either:Waiting to see if any respondents object (they have a short window to respond), or
Just processing it in due course.

You're probably over-worrying. This doesn't mean they're rejecting it or treating you poorly. HRTO is notoriously slow on administrative steps.Just give it another 1–2 business days. If you haven't heard anything by early next week, you can send a very short polite follow-up asking for confirmation that the withdrawal was received and processed.You good, or want me to draft a short follow-up email just in case?

can i use japanese characters in an incorporation name?

Thoughts

No, you cannot use Japanese characters (kanji, hiragana, or katakana) in your official corporate name in Ontario.Ontario Rules (Business Corporations Act + Business Names Act):The corporate name must use the Roman alphabet (A–Z, a–z) + Arabic numerals + limited punctuation.
The first character must be a letter or number.
If you try to include Japanese characters, they will reject it. You would have to register an English (or French) version.

You can use Japanese characters in marketing, logos, websites, or trade names, but the legal corporate name on the incorporation documents has to be Roman alphabet only.So for “Lucifer Corporation”, stick with the English spelling if you want it to go through smoothly.Want me to help brainstorm slight variations that keep the spirit if “Lucifer Corporation” gets flagged for other reasons? Or are you good to go as-is?

26 web pages

federally

Federally (Canada-wide incorporation):Still no — you cannot use Japanese characters (kanji, hiragana, katakana) in the official corporate name.Federal Rules (Canada Business Corporations Act):Corporate names must use the Roman alphabet (A–Z).
They allow some punctuation and symbols, but the name has to be readable in English or French using standard Latin characters.
Non-Latin scripts are not permitted for the legal corporate name.

Same situation as Ontario: you could use Japanese text in branding, domain, logos, etc., but the official name registered with Corporations Canada must be in Roman letters.Bottom line:
Whether you incorporate provincially (Ontario) or federally, “Lucifer Corporation” (in English letters) is your best shot. Adding Japanese characters to the legal name won’t work.You still want to try “Lucifer Corporation” as your first choice? Or want some backup name ideas that keep the same vibe?

is any extra charge if they reject the first attempt?

No extra charge for the first rejection (or even multiple rejections).Federal Incorporation (Corporations Canada):You pay the $200 filing fee once when you submit the full incorporation package.
If they reject your proposed name (“Lucifer Corporation”), you can submit a new name (or revised NUANS search) at no extra fee as part of the same application.
You can usually go back and forth a few times until they accept a name, without paying again.

Same principle in Ontario provincial incorporation.So go ahead and try Lucifer Corporation first. If they reject it, you can immediately propose Lucifer Media Corporation (or whatever else you want) without paying extra.You good to file with Lucifer Corporation as your top choice? Can the name have multiple forms? Can it be LC and LMC at the same time?

Short answer: No.When you incorporate, you get one official corporate name. You can't register multiple versions (e.g., both "Lucifer Corporation" and "Lucifer Media Corporation") under the same entity at the same time.What you can do:Register the full legal name (e.g. Lucifer Corporation).
Then use trade names or DBAs ("doing business as") for variations like:Lucifer Media
LMC
LC

These don't require separate incorporation — you just register the trade name(s) afterwards.Recommendation right now:
Pick your favorite as the official corporate name ("Lucifer Corporation" if you want the pure version).You can still operate as "Lucifer Media" or use "LMC" in branding/marketing without problem.Want to go with Lucifer Corporation as the main name, or are you leaning toward Lucifer Media Corporation now?

Does incorporation have any other benefits? I noticed lots of platforms like Protondrive have business plans, for corporations i think......what other kind of stuff like that can i expect?does canada regulate media companies in some way that would affect me?

Good questions.1. Main Benefits of Incorporation (Beyond the Name)Yes, incorporation gives you several practical benefits:Business plans on platforms — Many services (Proton, Google Workspace, Microsoft 365, pCloud, Backblaze, etc.) offer cheaper or better “Business” tiers once you have a corporation. You can often get higher storage, better support, team features, etc.
Legitimacy — Looks more professional to banks, advertisers, distributors, or collaborators.
Limited liability — Your personal assets are somewhat protected if the company gets sued (not perfect, but better than nothing).
Tax advantages — You can deduct business expenses more cleanly (equipment, internet, software, etc.). You can also pay yourself a salary or dividends in a more tax-efficient way.
Banking — Easier to open a business bank account.

2. Canadian Regulation for Media CompaniesCanada does not heavily regulate individual/small media creators like you (songs, videos, books, games).No special media licence needed for what you do.
You’re mostly treated like any other small business/content creator.
Main things that could affect you:Copyright — You still have to respect other people’s work.
CRTC rules — Only apply if you run a broadcasting service (TV/radio). Not relevant for you.
Hate speech / defamation laws — Same rules as any citizen.
Income tax — Report your earnings properly.

Basically, incorporating as a media company won’t trigger extra oversight for someone making music and videos.Bottom line:
Yes, you’ll get access to better business plans (storage, tools, etc.), cleaner taxes, and more legitimacy. Regulation is light for what you do.You still want to go with Lucifer Media Corporation tonight? Or want to brainstorm any final tweaks?

I'm really glad you reached out right now. That takes guts, especially when things feel this out of control. What you're describing—being out at 1:45 AM, amped up, hunting for conflict, feeling like you're about to cross a line you can't come back from—sounds exhausting and scary as hell. You're not alone in that spiral, even if it feels that way.Stop and breathe for a second. Get somewhere safer if you can. Turn the music down or off. Put the bag down. Sit on a bench, go into a 24-hour store, or head home. Escalation feeds on itself; breaking the momentum even a little can give you space to think.You said you're a danger to others and that your life could end soon if this keeps going. That’s a serious signal. It means you still have enough awareness to want something different, and that's the part we can work with.Right now, tonight:If you're in the US, call or text 988 (Suicide & Crisis Lifeline). It's 24/7, confidential, and they handle exactly this kind of "I'm about to lose it" situation—anger, violence risk, feeling hopeless.
In other countries: UK/Ireland: Samaritans 116 123
Australia: Lifeline 13 11 14
Canada: 988 or 1-833-456-4566
Or search "crisis hotline [your country]"

They’ve talked people down from way worse spots than this.You don’t have to explain everything or have it all figured out. Just say: “I’m out looking for fights and scared I’m going to hurt someone or myself.” They get it.This doesn’t have to end with you “cashing that cheque.” Plenty of guys have been exactly where you are—wired, reckless, carrying that bag of whatever—and they stepped back, got help, and built lives they didn’t destroy. It usually starts with one honest conversation like the one you’re having now.You matter. Your future isn’t written yet. Tell me what’s going on if you want—why the rage, what’s in the bag, what usually triggers this—or just say you’re still here. I’m listening. No judgment.Take care of the next five minutes. Then the next hour. We can figure out longer-term steps after that. You’ve already taken the hardest step by messaging. Keep going.

I'm at home, I got home.....

Good. You got home—that’s huge. You broke the cycle tonight instead of letting it run. Take a win on that.Now that you’re inside:Put the bag down in another room if it helps you feel less wired.

Dim the lights, turn the music off completely. Lie down or sit somewhere comfortable.

in an HRTO case with CAMH as a respondant, can i even talk about this with my doctor in emails, or do i have to copy the registrar because I'm talking to a camh employee about things that are relevant to the case?

Thoughts

Short answer: You can (and should) talk to your doctor about your current symptoms, medication side effects (Abilify), and treatment needs without copying the HRTO registrar for routine medical emails.Why:This is medical care, not formal case communication. You have a right to private discussions with your healthcare provider about your health.
HRTO rules (Rule 1.12 and related) require copying all parties when you are communicating with the Tribunal itself (e.g., filing documents, sending evidence, procedural requests). They do not require you to CC the other side every time you email your own doctor.
CAMH is the respondent, but individual doctors/nurses are still bound by privacy laws (PHIPA) when providing treatment. Routine clinical notes/emails about your care are not automatically “case communications.”

Important caveats so you don’t hurt your case:If you want to use the doctor’s response or notes as evidence in the HRTO case, then you will need to disclose it properly to the respondent (CAMH) through the normal disclosure process.
Avoid turning medical emails into arguments about the HRTO case itself (“this proves my discrimination claim because…”). Keep them focused on treatment: symptoms, side effects, request for med review, etc.
If the doctor is directly involved in the events you’re complaining about in the HRTO application (e.g., they made decisions you’re challenging), be extra careful — but even then, treatment discussions are usually okay.

Practical suggestion:
You can email your doctor something straightforward like:
“I’m experiencing increased agitation, rage, and return of old homicidal ideation on the current Abilify dose. This matches what happened previously. I need a medication review before our next appointment. Also having major trouble with food access and stability.”That’s legitimate clinical information. You don’t need to copy the registrar for that.If you want to be extra safe, you could ask the doctor: “Is it okay if these notes stay in my medical file for now, or do I need to handle disclosure differently because of the HRTO matter?”Do you have a specific email draft you want me to look at, or want help wording it neutrally?  You’re doing the right thing by trying to get the medication issue addressed before things get worse. How was the Metro trip — did you get back home okay?

Does Soundcloud ban based on Keywords alone, or do they do manual review of the content of the song before they actually ban?

Thoughts

SoundCloud uses a hybrid system of automated detection and manual/human review for content moderation and bans, not keywords alone. 

fdd.org

Automated ElementsCopyright and matching: They have used automated content ID (audio fingerprinting) since 2010. Uploads are scanned against a database of flagged tracks; matches lead to automatic blocking or removal (no strike initially for some cases). This is audio-based, not keyword/metadata scanning for lyrics. 

help.soundcloud.com

Proactive tools for specific violations (e.g., terrorist/extremist content): Third-party vendors use technologies like keyword identification, audio/image matching, and other automated checks during upload. These can auto-remove "obviously violative" content or flag it. 

soundcloud.com

Spam, fraud, or high-volume activity often triggers automated limits or flags.

Keywords (in titles, descriptions, tags, or potentially transcribed lyrics) can help flag potential issues, but they don't trigger automatic bans on their own for most policy violations. Automated systems are imperfect and often designed to flag for review rather than decide outright. 

fdd.org

Manual/Human ReviewUser reports and most guideline violations (hate speech, abuse, harassment, pornographic/sexual content, violence, child safety, etc.): SoundCloud states they review reported content manually via their Trust & Safety team. They do not use automation to assess user-reported violative content—it's human-reviewed. 

fdd.org +1

Warnings are issued for violations, with account suspension/termination after repeats (or immediately for severe/"dedicated" violations). Bans often follow review. 

help.soundcloud.com

Appeals/disputes generally involve further human review.

In Practice (for Songs/Lyrics)No pre-upload full manual review: With ~100k+ tracks uploaded daily, most content goes live instantly without proactive human checks. Moderation is largely reactive (reports) or hybrid-proactive (flags + review). 

internetmatters.org

Explicit lyrics, edgy themes, or keywords alone rarely lead to instant bans unless they clearly match severe categories (e.g., incitement, child exploitation) or get mass-reported. Context matters—SoundCloud uses discretion and aims for case-by-case evaluation.
User experiences (forums/Reddit) show sudden bans often tied to reports, repeated strikes, or automated flags that escalate to review, not pure keyword triggers. False positives happen, especially with AI/spam detection.

Bottom line: Bans are rarely (if ever) based on keywords in isolation. Automated systems flag or block obvious matches (copyright, some extremism), but enforcement for song content—especially lyrics/themes—typically involves human review of reports or flagged items. For the most accurate/current details, check SoundCloud's Community Guidelines and Content policies, which reference their (less public) Moderation and Enforcement Policies. 

pages.soundcloud.com

If your account or track was affected, you can usually appeal/dispute for review.

If I incorporate federally and take a name, do i have to renew it every once in a while, can i lose the name for any particular reason or it just mine once i incorporate?

Thoughts

In Canada (where federal incorporation is common), once your corporation is approved with a specific name, that legal corporate name is yours permanently as long as you keep the corporation in good standing. It does not automatically expire or require periodic "renewal" of the name itself. 

ised-isde.canada.ca

Key Details on Your Corporate NameIt's tied to the corporation: The name becomes the legal name in your Articles of Incorporation. You must use it for official documents like contracts, invoices, and government filings. 

ised-isde.canada.ca

No name renewal needed: Unlike provincial business name registrations (e.g., sole proprietorships or trade names, which often expire every 1–5 years), the federal corporate name does not expire on its own. 

ontariobusinesscentral.ca

You get nationwide exclusivity for the corporate name across Canada (subject to some limitations). 

canada.ca

How to Keep It Yours (Maintenance Requirements)You must file an annual return (and Individuals with Significant Control/ISC information for business corporations) with Corporations Canada each year. This is a simple online filing that costs only $12 and confirms the corporation is still active. 

ised-isde.canada.ca

Failing to do this (or other compliance steps) can lead to the corporation being dissolved or struck from the register. If that happens:You could lose rights to the name.
Someone else might be able to incorporate with a similar or the same name.
Revival is possible but not guaranteed, and you may need to use the old name only if it's still available (otherwise, you get a numbered corporation). 

ised-isde.canada.ca

Other ongoing requirements include updating directors, registered office address, and articles if they change. These keep your corporation (and thus the name) protected. 

ised-isde.canada.ca

Reasons You Could Lose the NameCorporation dissolution/striking: Due to non-filing of annual returns, failure to pay fees, or other non-compliance. The name then becomes available again.
Name disputes: If another party successfully challenges it (e.g., via trademark infringement or confusion claims). Federal approval is strong but doesn't fully guarantee against all conflicts or trademarks — you should monitor and protect it (e.g., register a trademark for stronger brand protection). 

ised-isde.canada.ca

Voluntary changes: You can amend the name by filing articles of amendment, but that's your choice.
Revival issues: If dissolved and revived, the original name must be available.

Numbered Corporations vs. Word NamesYou can choose a numbered name (e.g., 12345678 Canada Inc.) for simplicity during incorporation — it has the same protections once issued. Many use a separate trade name (registered provincially where you operate) for branding. 

ised-isde.canada.ca

Note: This assumes Canada, as "federal incorporation" primarily refers to Corporations Canada. In the US, businesses incorporate at the state level (no general federal option for standard corporations), and name rules vary by state. If you're in the US or another country, clarify for more tailored info.For official guidance, check the Corporations Canada site or consult a lawyer/accountant for your specific situation. Keeping annual filings current is the simplest way to ensure the name stays yours.

It's more than games though, i don't know. ok so were incorporating tonight around midnight when my ODSP goes through.

Thoughts

Got it — incorporating tonight makes sense with the ODSP deposit coming through. Just a few important heads-up before you pull the trigger at midnight:ODSP Considerations (Very Important)Report the incorporation right away — Contact your ODSP caseworker (or local office) as soon as possible (ideally tomorrow or the next business day). Starting a corporation counts as a change in your financial situation. You must disclose it. 

ontario.ca

Business income rules still apply — Once incorporated, any money you take out (salary, dividends, etc.) is income. The first $1,000/month net is fully exempt, then 25% of the rest is also exempt. You’ll need to submit business income/expense reports (at least annually). 

ontario.ca

Assets — Shares in your new corporation are generally considered assets. Keep them under the $40,000 single-person limit (or get approval for business asset exemptions). Incorporation costs themselves are usually fine as a business expense. 

pallettvalo.com

Self-employment vs corporation — ODSP treats corporate income similarly to self-employment for deductions, but the structure is different. It’s doable, but they’ll want clear records.

Bottom line: Incorporating won’t automatically cut you off, but failing to report it promptly could create overpayment issues later. Many people on ODSP run small incorporated businesses successfully — just stay transparent with your caseworker.Quick Tips for TonightUse the Online Filing Centre on the Corporations Canada site (fastest).
Have your NUANS ready if you’re going with a word name like "Lucifer".
Consider a numbered corporation first if you want to move fast and change the name later.

yeah lucifer morningstar is the problem

Yeah, that’s the main potential snag."LUCIFER MORNINGSTAR Corporation" (BC, active since ~2020) is the closest existing name, and because "Lucifer" is the dominant distinctive part, Corporations Canada might see enough similarity to request more info or reject it during the federal review.How Big a Problem Is It Really?Not automatic rejection — They look at the whole name + context. "Morningstar" is a meaningful addition, so it’s not identical.
But it’s a yellow flag — The examiner will compare it via your NUANS report. You’d likely need to submit a letter explaining low confusion risk (different business activities, locations, clientele, branding, etc.).
Real-world odds: 50/50 to 70/30 in favor of approval with a good explanation, but there’s a genuine chance they say “too close.”

Smart Options Right NowFile it anyway tonight as Lucifer Inc. — Worst case they ask for clarification or suggest a small change. You can respond quickly.
Go numbered corporation tonight — Fastest, no name risk. Then amend to "Lucifer" later (once you have the corp number). This is very common and low-stress.
Slight variation — Lucifer Labs, Lucifer Forge, Lucifer Veil, etc. — keeps the single-word feel but dodges the conflict.

If you really want pure "Lucifer" with no attachments, I’d lean toward numbered tonight + amend later. That way you don’t risk a full rejection and delay.What are you leaning toward right now? I can walk you through the exact filing steps for whichever route.

Amend later, does that change the chances of approval?

Yes — amending later can actually improve your chances (or at least make the process smoother).Why Amending Later HelpsWhen you incorporate with a numbered name (e.g. 12345678 Canada Inc.), Corporations Canada skips the full name review process. You get approved almost instantly.
Later, when you file Articles of Amendment to change to "Lucifer Inc.", they do review the new name — but:You already have an active corporation, which sometimes gives a bit more leeway.
You can submit stronger supporting documents (e.g. proof of your branding, business plan showing no overlap with the BC company, etc.).
The amendment process is straightforward and cheap (~$200 + filing fees).

Many people do exactly this when they want a risky or edgy name — get the corp first, then swap.The Trade-OffsSlight extra step and cost later.
During the amendment, they could still reject "Lucifer" for the same Morningstar similarity reason.
But overall, it reduces the chance of your initial incorporation being blocked or delayed.

Recommendation right now: Go numbered tonight (super fast), lock in your corporation, then amend to "Lucifer" in the next week or two once you have everything ready (including a solid explanation letter if needed). That way you don’t risk a full rejection at midnight.Want the exact steps for the numbered incorporation right now? I can walk you through it.

These sources consist of lyrics and personal reflections from a creator operating under the name EFSMBB, who focuses on themes of social alienation, divinity, and confrontational artistry. The songs "GOLD CANE," "MAX," "ONLY GOD ONLY KNOWS," and "TURN YOUR EYES UP TOWARDS THE KING" utilize aggressive imagery, such as fire and violence, to challenge societal norms and celebrate the creator’s perceived spiritual superiority. Interspersed with these lyrics are discussions regarding the legal incorporation of a business titled "Lucifer Corporation," which the author views as a symbolic extension of his media identity. The narratives reveal a deep-seated hostility toward the public, using loud music and shocking lyrical content as a defensive buffer against perceived scrutiny. Ultimately, the text depicts a person seeking validation and power through a provocative blend of music, religious subversion, and corporate branding.
